Global solutions of 2D isentropic compressible Navier-Stokes equations with one slow variable
Yong Lu, Ping Zhang

Abstract
. Motivated by [ 2 , 3 ] , we prove the global existence of solutions to the two-dimensional isentropic compressible Navier-Stokes equations with smooth initial data which are slowly varying in one direction and with initial density being away from vacuum. In particular, we present examples of initial data which generate unique global smooth solutions to 2D compressible Navier-Stokes equations with constant viscosity and with initial data which are neither small perturbation of some constant equilibrium state nor of small energy.



Keywords:

compressible Navier-Stokes equations, strong solution, large data, slow variable.

AMS Subject Classification (2000):
35Q35, 35B65, 76N10.

1.
Introduction

We investigate the global existence of smooth solutions to the following
two-dimensional isentropic compressible Navier-Stokes equations describing the motion of viscous barotropic compressible flows:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\rho+{\rm div\,}(\rho u)=0,\qquad(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times{\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R},\\
&\partial_{t}(\rho u)+{\rm div\,}(\rho u\otimes u)-\mu\Delta u-\nabla\left(\mu^{\prime}{\rm div\,}u\right)+\nabla p(\rho)=0,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\rho(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
u(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
designate the the density and the velocity field of the fluid respectively.
Here the shear viscosity
\mu
and bulk viscosity
\mu^{\prime}
are assumed to be constant with
\mu>0
and
\mu+\mu^{\prime}>0
. The pressure function
p

is a smooth non-decreasing function of the density
\rho.
For simplicity, we just consider power law pressure with

(1.2)

p(\rho)=a\rho^{\gamma},\quad a>0,\quad\gamma\geq 1.

The typical range of the adiabatic constant is
1<\gamma\leq\frac{5}{3}
where the maximum value
\gamma=\frac{5}{3}
is related to monatomic gases, the intermedius value
\gamma=\frac{7}{5}
is related to diatomic gases including air, and lower values close to
1
is related to polyatomic gases at high temperature. In this paper, we allow any
\gamma\in[1,2]
. One may check
[
19
]
for more background of the system (
1.1
).

The global existence of smooth solution to the multi-dimensional compressible Navier-Stokes equations is one of the most important
problems in the theory which describes the motion of the viscous compressible fluids. In
[
20
]
, Matsumura and Nishida proved the global existence of solutions to the full compressible Navier-Stokes equations when the initial data is close to a constant equilibrium state in
H^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Danchin
[
4
]
worked out similar result yet with initial data in certain optimal function spaces. Huang, Li and Xin
[
12
]
established the global existence and uniqueness of classical solutions to the Cauchy problem of the barotropic compressible Navier-Stokes equations in three space dimensions with smooth initial data which are of small energy but possibly large oscillations. Concerning general smooth data without size restriction and with initial density being away from vacuum, the first well-posedness result is due to Vaĭgant and Kazhikhov
[
30
]
where the authors proved the global unique solution to the two-dimensional compressible Navier-Stokes system (
1.1
) in
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
with the bulk viscosity
\mu^{\prime}=b\rho^{\beta}
and
b>0,\beta>3.
Lately this result was improved by Huang and Li
[
13
]
for
\beta>\frac{4}{3}.
For further results in higher dimensional setting, we refer to
[
31
,
1
,
9
]
and the references therein.

On the other hand, Chemin and Gallagher
[
2
]
proved the global existence of smooth solution to three-dimensional incompressible Navier-Stokes equations
(NS)
with initial data which is slowly varying in one direction. This type of result was extended by Chemin and the second author
[
3
]

to the three-dimensional incompressible inhomogeneous Navier-Stokes equations and by Liu and the second author
[
17
]
for
(NS)
with unidirectional derivative of the initial velocity being sufficiently small in some critical functional space.

Motivated by
[
2
,
3
,
17
]
, we are looking for global solutions of (
1.1
) that are periodic in
x
variable, and have slow variation in
y
variable.
This means that we implement the system (
1.1
) with initial data of the form

(1.3)

\rho(0,x,y)=[\varsigma_{0}]_{\varepsilon}(x,y),\quad u(0,x,y)=\big([w_{0}]_{\varepsilon}(x,y),[\mathfrak{w}_{0}]_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\big),

where
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R}
with
{\mathbb{T}}
being the torus
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. Here and in all that follows, we shall always denote

[f]_{\varepsilon}(x,y)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}f(x,\varepsilon y).

We aim at showing the global well-posedness of (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) without assuming any size restriction on
(\varsigma_{0},w_{0},\mathfrak{w}_{0})

provided that
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small.
We remark that data of the form (
1.3
) are neither of small perturbation of some constant equilibrium state, say
(1,0),
nor of small energy. Hence in particular, our result gives examples indicating that 2D compressible Navier-Stokes equations with constant viscosity might
be globally well-posed even for general initial data with initial density being away from vacuum.

Formally with initial data given by (
1.3
), we may seek solution of (
1.1
) as follows

\displaystyle\rho(t,x,y)=[{\xi}]_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y),\quad u(t,x,y)=[v]_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y),

then it follows from (
1.1
) that

(1.4)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\xi+{\rm div}_{\varepsilon}(\xi v)=0,\\
&\partial_{t}(\xi v)+{\rm div}_{\varepsilon}(\xi v\otimes v)-\mu\Delta_{\varepsilon}v-\mu^{\prime}\nabla_{\varepsilon}{\rm div}_{\varepsilon}v+\nabla_{\varepsilon}p(\xi)=0,\end{aligned}\right.

with initial data

\displaystyle\xi(0,x,y)=\varsigma_{0}(x,y),\quad v(0,x,y)=\big(w_{0}(x,y),\mathfrak{w}_{0}(x,y)\big).

Here

(1.5)

\nabla_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\\
\varepsilon\partial_{y}\end{pmatrix},\quad{\rm div}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\nabla_{\varepsilon}\cdot\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Delta_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\partial_{x}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{y}^{2}.

Formally passing
\varepsilon\to 0
in (
1.4
) leads to a limit system of the form with a parameter
y:

(1.6)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\eta+\partial_{x}(\eta w)=0,\qquad(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times{\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R},\\
&\eta(\partial_{t}w+w\partial_{x}w)-\nu\partial_{x}^{2}w-\partial_{x}p(\eta)=0\quad\hbox{with}\quad\nu\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\mu+\mu^{\prime},\end{aligned}\right.

and

(1.7)

\eta(\partial_{t}\mathfrak{w}+w\partial_{x}\mathfrak{w})-\mu\partial_{x}^{2}\mathfrak{w}=0,\qquad(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times{\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R}.

Accordingly, the initial data are

(1.8)

\eta(0,x,y)=\varsigma_{0}(x,y),\quad w(0,x,y)=w_{0}(x,y),\quad\mathfrak{w}(0,x,y)=\mathfrak{w}_{0}(x,y).

We observe that the equations of
(\eta,w)
and the equation of
\mathfrak{w}
are decoupled. Furthermore,

(\eta,w)
satisfies one dimensional compressible Navier-Stokes equations with a parameter
y.
We shall study in detail about this system in Section
3
.

Before proceeding, we assume that

(1.9)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\underline{\varsigma}_{0}\leq\varsigma_{0}\leq\bar{\varsigma}_{0},\quad(\partial_{y}^{j}(\varsigma_{0}-1),\partial_{y}^{j}w_{0},\partial_{y}^{j}\mathfrak{w}_{0})\in(L_{{\rm v}}^{2}\cap L_{{\rm v}}^{\infty})(H_{{\rm h}}^{5})\quad\hbox{with}\quad

\displaystyle A_{k}(y)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\sum_{j=0}^{2}\|\partial_{y}^{j}(\varsigma_{0}-1,w_{0},\mathfrak{w}_{0})(\cdot,y)\|_{H^{k}_{\rm h}}^{2}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\bar{A}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}A_{k}(y),\quad 0\leq k\leq 5,

for some positive constants
\underline{\varsigma}_{0}
and
\bar{\varsigma}_{0}
, and for
j=0,1,2
. Here the subscript
{\rm h}
(resp.
{{\rm v}}
) denotes the norm on
{\mathbb{T}}_{x}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{y}
). We assume moreover that

(1.10)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\varsigma_{0}(x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}=1,\quad\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\varsigma_{0}w_{0})(x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\varsigma_{0}\mathfrak{w}_{0})(x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}=0,\quad\forall\,y\in\mathbb{R}.

Our first result is concerned with the large time exponential decay for the solutions to the equations (
1.6
) and (
1.7
)
with initial data (
1.8
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(\varsigma_{0},w_{0},\mathfrak{w}_{0})
satisfy (
1.9
) and (
1.10
). Then for each
y\in\mathbb{R}
,
the system (
1.6
)–(
1.8
) has a unique global-in-time strong solution
(\eta,w,\mathfrak{w})\in C([0,\infty);H^{2}({\mathbb{T}}))
so that

(1.11)

\underline{\eta}\leq\eta(t,x,y)\leq\bar{\eta},\quad\mbox{for some positive constants $\underline{\eta}$ and $\bar{\eta}$.}

Moreover,
there exist positive constants
C
and
\alpha
solely depending on
(a,\gamma,\nu,\bar{\varsigma}_{0},\underline{\varsigma}_{0},\bar{A}_{4})
such that
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}

(1.12)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\Bigl(\|(\eta-1)(t,\cdot,y)\|_{H^{4}_{\rm h}}+\|(w,\mathfrak{w})(t,\cdot,y)\|_{H^{5}_{\rm h}}+\|(\eta_{t},w_{t},\mathfrak{w}_{t})(t,\cdot,y)\|_{H^{3}_{\rm h}}

\displaystyle+\|(\eta_{tt},w_{tt},\mathfrak{w}_{tt})(t,\cdot,y)\|_{H^{1}_{\rm h}}\Bigr)+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\Bigl(\|\partial_{y}^{j}\eta\|_{H^{3-j}_{\rm h}}+\|\partial_{y}^{j}(w,\mathfrak{w})(t,\cdot,y)\|_{H^{4-j}_{\rm h}}

\displaystyle+\|\partial_{y}^{j}(\eta_{t},w_{t},\mathfrak{w}_{t})(t,\cdot,y)\|_{H^{2-j}_{\rm h}}\Bigr)\leq CA_{5}^{\frac{1}{2}}(y)e^{-\alpha t}.

We point out that to prove Theorem
1.1
,
it is crucial to establish the related estimates for
(\eta,w)
in (
1.12
).
Indeed with thus obtained estimates for
(\eta,w)
,
those estimates for
\mathfrak{w}
follow immediately. The proof of Theorem
1.1
will be
presented from Section
3
to Section
6
.

The main result of this paper states as follows:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(\varsigma_{0},w_{0},\mathfrak{w}_{0})
satisfy (
1.9
) and (
1.10
). Then
(
1.1
)–(
1.3
) has a unique global-in-time strong solution
(\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})\in C([0,\infty);H^{2}({\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R})).

Furthermore, let
(\eta,w,\mathfrak{w})
be the global solution to (
1.6
–
1.8
)
obtained in Theorem
1.1
, we denote

(1.13)

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\rho_{\varepsilon}-[\eta]_{\varepsilon}\quad\hbox{and}\quad R_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}u_{\varepsilon}-\left([w]_{\varepsilon},[\mathfrak{w}]_{\varepsilon}\right)^{\rm T},

and the energy functional

(1.14)

\displaystyle E_{\varepsilon}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}

\displaystyle\sup_{0<t<T}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R}}\left(|R_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|\varrho_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|\nabla\omega_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R}}\left(|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|\nabla\omega_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\ |\nabla\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t,

\displaystyle\theta_{\varepsilon}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}

\displaystyle\sup_{0<t<T}\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R})},

where
\omega_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}curlR_{\varepsilon}=\partial_{y}R_{\varepsilon}^{1}-\partial_{x}R_{\varepsilon}^{2}
,
\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\partial_{t}R_{\varepsilon}+u_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla R_{\varepsilon}
. Then there exists a constant
C
solely depending on
(a,\gamma,\mu,\mu^{\prime},\bar{\varsigma}_{0},\underline{\varsigma}_{0},\|A_{5}\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{y})})
such that

(1.15)

\displaystyle E_{\varepsilon}(\infty)+\theta_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\infty)\leq C\varepsilon.

We remark that the main idea used to prove Theorem
1.2
is to approximate 2D compressible Navier-Stokes equations with a slow variable via 1D compressible Navier-Stokes equations with a parameter. This is inspired by the study in
[
2
,
3
]
where 3D incompressible Navier-Stokes equations with a slow variable can be well approximated by 2D Navier-Stokes equations which is globally well-posed.

Let us end this section with some notations that we shall use throughout this paper.

Notations:
In the whole paper,
we designate
\Omega\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}{\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R},

Q_{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}(0,T)\times\Omega
and

Q_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}(0,+\infty)\times\Omega.

We shall always denote
C
to be a uniform constant
which may vary from line to line, and
a\lesssim b
means that
a\leq Cb
, and
a\thicksim b

means that both
a\leq Cb
and
b\leq Ca
hold.
And we use subscript
{\rm h}
(resp.
{{\rm v}}
) to denote the norm taken
on
{\mathbb{T}}_{x}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{y}
).

2.
Ideas and structure of the proof

In this section, we shall sketch the main ideas of the proof to Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
.

For each fixed
y\in\mathbb{R}
, the global existence and uniqueness of strong solutions to 1D compressible Navier-Stokes equations (
1.6
) is well known (see
for instance
[
14
,
16
,
24
,
25
,
26
]
).
Although the results in the above references focus on domains either being the whole line
\mathbb{R}
or being a bounded interval,
the well-posedness results can be easily modified to the torus
{\mathbb{T}}
. More precisely, under the assumptions that

(2.1)

0<\underline{\varsigma}_{0}\leq\varsigma_{0}\leq\bar{\varsigma}_{0}<\infty,\quad\varsigma_{0}-1\in H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}),\quad w_{0}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}),

(
1.6
)–(
1.8
) has a unique global solution
(\eta,w)
so that for each
T>0

(2.2)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle 0<\underline{\varsigma}(T)\leq\eta\leq\bar{\varsigma}(T)<\infty,\quad\eta-1\in C([0,T];H^{1}({\mathbb{T}})),\quad\eta_{t}\in C([0,T];L^{2}({\mathbb{T}})),

\displaystyle w\in C([0,T];H^{1}({\mathbb{T}})),\quad w\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}({\mathbb{T}})),\quad\hbox{and}\quad w_{t}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}({\mathbb{T}})).

In general, the related estimates in (
2.2
) depend on the time interval
[0,T]
. We shall show below that
such estimates hold uniformly in time. Actually, we shall show exponential decay-in-time estimates of the solutions. In the case when spatial domain is a bounded interval and
with homogeneous
boundary condition for
w,
Straškraba and Zlotnik
[
26
]
established exponential decay-in-time estimate for the
H^{1}
norms of
\eta-1
and
w
. It is not obvious that the same exponential decay estimate holds for the case of torus uniformly in
y\in\mathbb{R}
. One difficulty lies in that, unlike the case in bounded domain with homogeneous boundary condition for
w,
we can not have a similar version of Poincaré inequality on torus
{\mathbb{T}}
.
Another difficulty is due to the presence of the parameter
y\in\mathbb{R}.
We will have to show that the corresponding decay estimates are uniform with respect to
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
(\partial_{y}^{j}\eta,\partial_{y}^{j}w),

j=1,2,
share the same decay estimate. To achieve this, we need to show the density function
\eta
admits a uniform finite upper bound and a uniform positive lower bound with respect to
y\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular, in order to derive the uniform strictly positive lower bound, one needs to show that as time goes to infinity, the kinetic energy goes to zero and the integral of the pressure goes to a strictly positive number with a speed independent of
y\in\mathbb{R}
. The result in
[
26
]

can not be directly applied here.
Instead, we shall first establish the uniform upper bound of the density by using the idea in
[
26
]
. Then we find that this is enough to derive the exponential decay for the basic energy, see Proposition
3.2
. Since
(\partial_{y}^{j}\eta,\partial_{y}^{j}w),

j=1,2,
does not fulfill a typical 1D compressible Navier-Stokes system, there arise new difficulties in this procedure of deriving the exponential decay estimates for
(\partial_{y}^{j}\eta,\partial_{y}^{j}w),

j=1,2
. A further difficulty comes from the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality in
\Omega
. Since
\Omega={\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R}
which is essentially bounded in
x
variable, thus the classical Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality reads (
p>2
):

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{2}{p}}\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{1-\frac{2}{p}}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{2}{p}}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{1-\frac{2}{p}},\quad\forall f\in H^{1}(\Omega).

However, it happens to us that we can control the integral of
\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}
in time variable over
\mathbb{R}^{+}
for some
f
, but not for the quantity
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2},
see for instance (
2.34
) and (
2.35
) on the effective viscous flux in the proof of Lemma
2.3
. So we need a modified version of Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality in
\Omega
which always involves the term
\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},
which is Lemma
2.1
below.

In Section
3
, we shall present the detailed decay-in-time estimates for solutions of (
1.6
)–(
1.8
).

Proposition 2.1
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, (
1.6
)–(
1.8
) has a unique global solution
(\eta,w)
and there exist positive constants
\underline{\eta},\bar{\eta},

C
and
\alpha
solely depending on
(a,\gamma,\nu,\bar{\varsigma}_{0},\underline{\varsigma}_{0},\bar{A}_{4})
such that (
1.11
) and

(2.3)

\displaystyle\|(\eta-1)(t,\cdot,y)\|_{H^{4}_{\rm h}}+\|w(t,\cdot,y)\|_{H^{5}_{\rm h}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\bigl\|(\partial_{t}^{i}\eta,\partial_{t}^{i}w)(t,\cdot,y)\bigr\|_{H^{5-2i}_{\rm h}}\leq CA_{5}^{\frac{1}{2}}(y)e^{-\alpha t}.

hold for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}.

To avoid notational complexity, we shall denote
E(y)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
to a universal function, which is determined by the initial conditions. The positive constants
C
and
\alpha
are also determined by the initial conditions. From now on, we shall not point out the precise dependence of
E(y)
and the constants
C,\alpha,
and we neglect the dependence on the
y
variable.

Proposition 2.2
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, there exist positive constants
C
and
\alpha
so that
the global solution
(\eta,w)
of (
1.6
)–(
1.8
) verifies

(2.4)

\displaystyle\|\eta_{y}(t)\|_{H^{2}_{\rm h}}+\|w_{y}(t)\|_{H^{3}_{\rm h}}+\|(\eta_{yt},w_{yt})(t)\|_{H^{1}_{\rm h}}\leq CE^{\frac{1}{2}}(y)e^{-\alpha t},\quad\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\ y\in\mathbb{R}.

Proposition 2.3
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, there exist positive constants
C
and
\alpha
so that the global solution
(\eta,w)
of (
1.6
)–(
1.8
) fulfills

(2.5)

\displaystyle\|\eta_{yy}(t)\|_{H^{1}_{\rm h}}+\|w_{yy}(t)\|_{H^{2}_{\rm h}}+\|(\eta_{yyt},w_{yyt})(t)\|_{L^{2}_{\rm h}}\leq E^{\frac{1}{2}}(y)e^{-\alpha t},\quad\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\ y\in\mathbb{R}.

Proposition 2.4
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, (
1.7
)–(
1.8
) has a unique global solution

\mathfrak{w}
and there exists positive constants
C
and
\alpha
so that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and

y\in\mathbb{R}

(2.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2}\bigl(\|\partial_{t}^{j}\mathfrak{w}(t)\|_{H^{5-2j}_{\rm h}}+\|\partial_{y}^{j}\mathfrak{w}(t)\|_{H^{4-j}_{\rm h}}+\|\partial_{y}^{j}\mathfrak{w}_{t}(t)\|_{H^{2-j}_{\rm h}}\bigr)\leq CE^{\frac{1}{2}}(y)e^{-\alpha t}\quad\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\ y\in\mathbb{R}.

The proof of Proposition
2.2
will be presented in Section
4
. While we shall outline the proof of Propositions
2.3
and
2.4
in Sections
5

and
6
respectively. By combing Propositions
2.1
-
2.4
, we conclude the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Let
(\eta,w,\mathfrak{w})
be the unique global solution of (
1.6
)-(
1.8
), which has been
constructed in Theorem
1.1
. We define

(2.7)

\rho^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}[\eta]_{\varepsilon},\quad u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}([w]_{\varepsilon},[\mathfrak{w}]_{\varepsilon})^{\rm T}.

Then in view of (
1.6
) and (
1.7
), we have

(2.8)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\rho^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}+{\rm div\,}(\rho^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon})=\varepsilon[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon},\\
&\rho^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}(\partial_{t}u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}+u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon})-\mu\Delta u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}-\mu^{\prime}\nabla{\rm div\,}u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla p(\rho^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon})=-G_{\varepsilon},\end{aligned}\right.

where
G_{\varepsilon}=(G_{1,\varepsilon},G_{2,\varepsilon})^{\rm T}
is given by

(2.9)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle G_{1,\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}-\varepsilon[\eta\mathfrak{w}w_{y}]_{\varepsilon}+\mu\varepsilon^{2}[w_{yy}]_{\varepsilon}+\mu^{\prime}\varepsilon[\mathfrak{w}_{xy}]_{\varepsilon},

\displaystyle G_{2,\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}-\varepsilon[\eta\mathfrak{w}\mathfrak{w}_{y}]_{\varepsilon}+\nu\varepsilon^{2}[\mathfrak{w}_{yy}]_{\varepsilon}+\mu^{\prime}\varepsilon[w_{xy}]_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon[p(\eta)_{y}]_{\varepsilon}.

Then by virtue of (
1.12
), we deduce that

(2.10)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}G_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\alpha t},\quad\|G_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\alpha t},\quad\|G_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\varepsilon e^{-\alpha t}.

On the other hand, with initial data given by (
1.3
) and
(\varsigma_{0},w_{0},\mathfrak{w}_{0})
satisfying (
1.9
) and (
1.10
),
there exists a positive time
T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}>0
so that (
1.1
)–(
1.3
) has a unique solution
(\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})
on

[0,T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon})
and for any
T<T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon},

(2.11)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle 0<\underline{\rho}(T)\leq\rho_{\varepsilon}\leq\bar{\rho}(T)<\infty,\quad\rho_{\varepsilon}\in C([0,T];H^{2}(\Omega)),\quad\partial_{t}\rho_{\varepsilon}\in C([0,T];H^{1}(\Omega)),

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}\in C([0,T];H^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{3}(\Omega)),\quad\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega)).

One may check
[
21
,
23
]
for details.

Let
T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}
be the maximal existence time of
(\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})
so that (
2.11
) holds. We are going to prove that

T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}=\infty
for
\varepsilon
being sufficiently small. In order to do so, we define the remaining term

(2.12)

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\rho_{\varepsilon}-\rho^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}\quad\hbox{and}\quad R_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}u_{\varepsilon}-u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}.

Recall that
\mathfrak{D}_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\partial_{t}+u_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla.
Then it follows from (
1.1
) and (
2.8
) that

(2.13)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\varrho_{\varepsilon}+{\rm div\,}(\rho_{\varepsilon}R_{\varepsilon})+{\rm div\,}(\varrho_{\varepsilon}u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon})=-\varepsilon[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon},\\
&\rho_{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}-\mu\Delta R_{\varepsilon}-\mu^{\prime}\nabla{\rm div\,}R_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\big(p(\rho_{\varepsilon})-p(\rho^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon})\big)\\
&\qquad+\rho_{\varepsilon}R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}+\varrho_{\varepsilon}(\partial_{t}u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}+u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon})=G_{\varepsilon},\\
&\varrho_{\varepsilon}(0,x,y)=0,\quad R_{\varepsilon}(0,x,y)=0.\end{aligned}\right.

We define

(2.14)

\displaystyle T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\sup\big\{T<T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon};\ \theta_{\varepsilon}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\sup_{0<t<T}\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{1,\underline{\eta}\right\}\big\}.

It is easy to observe that for all
t<T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
:

(2.15)

\displaystyle 0<{\underline{\eta}}/{2}\leq\rho_{\varepsilon}\leq\bar{\eta}+{\underline{\eta}}/{2}<\infty.

We shall first derive the basic energy estimate for
(\varrho_{\varepsilon},R_{\varepsilon})
for
t<T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}.

A standard way to perform this estimate is to test the momentum type equation in (
2.13
) by
R_{\varepsilon}
and using integration by parts. A tricky term to handle is the one related to the pressure:

(2.16)

\int_{\Omega}\big(p(\rho_{\varepsilon})-p(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})\big){\rm div\,}R_{\varepsilon}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}.

Similar term
p(\rho_{\varepsilon}){\rm div\,}u_{\varepsilon}
appears in the renormalized formulation to the continuity equation of the
compressible Navier-Stokes equations:

\partial_{t}P(\rho_{\varepsilon})+{\rm div\,}(P(\rho_{\varepsilon})u_{\varepsilon})+(\rho_{\varepsilon}P^{\prime}(\rho_{\varepsilon})-P(\rho_{\varepsilon})){\rm div\,}u_{\varepsilon}=0,

where
P(\rho)
is called the pressure potential, which is determined by

\rho P^{\prime}(\rho)-P(\rho)=p(\rho).

As a result, it comes out

\int_{\Omega}p(\rho_{\varepsilon}){\rm div\,}u_{\varepsilon}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}=-\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{\Omega}P(\rho_{\varepsilon})\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}.

In particular, for our case
p(\rho)=a\rho^{\gamma}
with
\gamma\geq 1
, the corresponding pressure potential is

(2.17)

P(\rho)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\frac{1}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma}\ \mbox{if $\gamma>1$},\quad P(\rho)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}a(\rho\log\rho+1)\ \mbox{if $\gamma=1$}.

However, it is not clear at a first glance how to use such an argument to deal with the term (
2.16
). Here we shall employ the well-known relative entropy inequality to derive the basic energy estimate for
\varrho_{\varepsilon}
and
R_{\varepsilon}
.
Relative entropy inequality is widely used in the study of uniqueness and stability for Navier–Stokes equations and some related models, see
[
7
,
10
]
for compressible Navier–Stokes equations,
[
8
]
for compressible Navier–Stokes–Fourier equations, and
[
18
]
for a compressible Oldroyd model. The result states as follows:

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
(\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})
be the local-in-time strong solution of (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) satisfying (
2.11
). For each pair
(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{u})
satisfying the same regularity assumption as that of
(\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})
listed in (
2.11
), we define the
relative energy functional

(2.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\bigl((\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})|(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{u})\bigr)(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\int_{\Omega}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\rho_{\varepsilon}|u_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{u}|^{2}+P(\rho_{\varepsilon})-P(\tilde{\rho})-P^{\prime}(\tilde{\rho})(\rho_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{\rho})\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y},

where the pressure potential
P
is defined by (
2.17
). Then for any
t\in(0,T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon})
, the following
relative entropy equality
holds

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\bigl((\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})|(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{u})\bigr)(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\mu\left|\nabla(u_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{u})\right|^{2}+

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}|{\rm div\,}(u_{\varepsilon}-\tilde{u})|^{2}\bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}\bigl((\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})|_{t=0}|(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{u})|_{t=0}\bigr)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}(t^{\prime})\,{\rm d}t^{\prime},

where

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\int_{\Omega}\rho_{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{D}_{t}\tilde{u}\cdot(\tilde{u}-u_{\varepsilon})\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}+\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\mu\nabla\tilde{u}:\nabla(\tilde{u}-u_{\varepsilon})+\mu^{\prime}{\rm div\,}\tilde{u}\,{\rm div\,}(\tilde{u}-u_{\varepsilon})\bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}\bigl((\tilde{\rho}-\rho_{\varepsilon})\partial_{t}P^{\prime}(\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon})+(\tilde{\rho}\tilde{u}-\rho_{\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla P^{\prime}(\tilde{\rho})\bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}-\int_{\Omega}{\rm div\,}\tilde{u}(p(\rho_{\varepsilon})-p(\tilde{\rho}))\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}.

There are also similar relative entropy inequalities related to finite energy weak solutions of different models: for instance, Theorem 2.4 of
[
7
]
for the compressible Navier–Stokes equations and Proposition 5.3 of
[
18
]
for a compressible Oldroyd-B model. The proof of Proposition
2.5
follows the same line as the argument in
[
7
]
or
[
18
]
, and we skip the details here.

Thanks to Proposition
2.5
, we shall prove in Section
7.1
that

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
T_{\varepsilon}^{\star}
be given by (
2.14
). Then for all
t<T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
, one has

(2.21)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\bigl(|R_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{2}\bigr)(t)\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t^{\prime}\leq C\varepsilon,

where the positive constants
C
is independent of
T^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
.

In Section
7.4
, we shall prove the energy estimate for the derivatives of
R_{\varepsilon}.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let the energy functional
E_{\varepsilon}(T)
be given by (
1.14
). Then for all
T\leq T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},
one has

(2.22)

E_{\varepsilon}(T)\leq C\varepsilon+C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\bigl(|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}|^{3}+|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}|^{4}\bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t.

In order to close the energy estimate, (
2.22
), we need to handle the estimates of the cubic and quadratic terms:

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}|^{3}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t^{\prime}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}|^{4}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t^{\prime}.

Here we introduce the following refined Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality in
\Omega
:

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
2<p<\infty
, there exists a constant
C
depending solely on
p
such that for all
f\in H^{1}(\Omega)
there holds

(2.23)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\Bigl(\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{2}{p}}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{1-\frac{2}{p}}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p}}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\Bigr).

The proof of Lemma
2.1
will be presented in Appendix
B
.

Motivated by
[
15
,
19
]
, we define the effective viscous flux
\mathfrak{F}
for the system (
2.13
) as follows

(2.24)

\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\nu{\rm div\,}R_{\varepsilon}-\big(p(\rho_{\varepsilon})-p(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})\big).

Lemma 2.2
.

For
T\leq T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},
one has

(2.25)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\rho_{\varepsilon}-\rho^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}|^{6}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t\leq C\varepsilon^{3}+C\varepsilon^{2}E_{\varepsilon}(T)+C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}|^{6}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t.

Proof.

In view of the continuity equations of (
1.1
) and (
2.13
), we write

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{t}(\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon})+R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\log\rho^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}+{\rm div\,}R_{\varepsilon}=-\varepsilon(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{-1}[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon},

which together with (
2.24
) implies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{t}(\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})=-R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}-\nu^{-1}\bigl(\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}+\big(p(\rho_{\varepsilon})-p(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})\big)\bigr)-\varepsilon(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{-1}[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon}.

Multiplying the above equation by
6(\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{5}
and then applying Young’s inequality gives

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{t}(\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{6}+6\nu^{-1}\big(p(\rho_{\varepsilon})-p(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})\big)(\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{5}

\displaystyle=-5\left(R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}+\nu^{-1}\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{-1}[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon}\right)(\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{5}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\delta}\left(|R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}|^{6}+|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}|^{6}+|\varepsilon(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{-1}[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon}|^{6}\right)+\delta|\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}|^{6}.

Notice that for
t<T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},
there holds (
2.15
) so that

\displaystyle 6\nu^{-1}\big(p(\rho_{\varepsilon})-p(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})\big)(\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{5}\geq 2C\delta|\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}|^{6}\geq\delta|\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}|^{6}+\delta(\rho_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{6},

for some small positive constant
\delta
and some
C\geq 2
. We thus obtain

(2.26)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{t}(\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{6}+\delta(\rho_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{6}\leq C_{\delta}\left(|R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}|^{6}+|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}|^{6}+|\varepsilon(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{-1}[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon}|^{6}\right).

Let us introduce the particle trajectory
X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y)
of
u_{\varepsilon}
via

(2.27)

\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y)=u_{\varepsilon}(t,X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y));\quad X_{\varepsilon}(0,x,y)=(x,y).

Then we deduce from (
2.26
) that

(2.28)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\bigl(\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\bigr)^{6}(t,X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y))+\delta\bigl(\rho_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\bigr)^{6}(t,X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y))

\displaystyle\leq C_{\delta}\left(|R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}|^{6}+|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}|^{6}+\varepsilon^{6}|(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{-1}[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon}|^{6}\right)(t,X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y)).

Let
T\leq T_{\varepsilon}^{\star}
. Integrating the above inequality over
(0,T)\times\Omega
yields

(2.29)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\bigr)^{6}(T,X_{\varepsilon}(T,x,y))\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}+\delta\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}(\rho_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{6}(t,X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y)){\rm d}{x}{\rm d}{y}{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\bigl(|R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}|^{6}(t,X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y))+|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}|^{6}(t,X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y))\bigr){\rm d}{x}{\rm d}{y}{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+C\varepsilon^{6}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{-1}[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon}|^{6}(t,X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y))\,{\rm d}{x}{\rm d}{y}{\rm d}t.

Recall (
2.15
) and Lemma 3.2 of
[
15
]
that there exists
C
depending only on the positive lower bound and upper bound of
\rho_{\varepsilon}
such that for any nonnegative smooth integrable function
g,
there holds for all
t<T_{\varepsilon}^{\star}
,

\displaystyle C^{-1}\int_{\Omega}g(t,x,y){\rm d}{x}{\rm d}{y}\leq\int_{\Omega}g(t,X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y)){\rm d}{x}{\rm d}{y}\leq C\int_{\Omega}g(t,x,y){\rm d}{x}{\rm d}{y}.

We thus deduce from (
1.11
) and (
2.29
) that

(2.30)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}(\rho_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{6}(t,x,y){\rm d}{x}{\rm d}{y}{\rm d}t\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}|^{6}(t,x,y){\rm d}{x}{\rm d}{y}{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}|^{6}(t,x,y){\rm d}{x}{\rm d}{y}{\rm d}t+C\varepsilon^{6}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{-1}[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon}|^{6}(t,x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}{\rm d}{y}{\rm d}t.

It follows from Theorem
1.1
, Proposition
2.6
and Lemma
2.1
that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}|^{6}(t,x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|R_{\varepsilon}|^{6}e^{-\alpha t}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\alpha t}\big(\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{4}+\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{4}\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\big){\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{0<t<T}\big(\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{4}\big)\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\alpha t}\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}(E_{\varepsilon}(T)+\varepsilon),

and

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{6}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{-1}[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon}|^{6}(t,x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq\underline{\eta}^{-1}\varepsilon^{6}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\varepsilon^{-1}|(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}|^{6}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{5}\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\alpha t}{\rm d}t\leq C\varepsilon^{5}.

By inserting the above estimates into (
2.30
), we conclude the proof of (
2.25
).
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

For
T\leq T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},
there holds

(2.31)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}|^{6}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t\leq C\varepsilon^{3}+C\varepsilon^{2}E_{\varepsilon}(T)+C\varepsilon E^{2}_{\varepsilon}(T).

Proof.

Recall that
\omega_{\varepsilon}=\partial_{y}R^{1}_{\varepsilon}-\partial_{x}R^{2}_{\varepsilon},
we observe that

\Delta R_{\varepsilon}=\nabla{\rm div\,}R_{\varepsilon}+\nabla^{\perp}\omega_{\varepsilon},\quad\nabla^{\perp}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{y}\\
-\partial_{x}\end{pmatrix},

which together with (
2.24
)
implies

\displaystyle\nu\Delta R_{\varepsilon}=\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\big(p(\rho_{\varepsilon})-p(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})\big)+\nu\nabla^{\perp}\omega_{\varepsilon}.

Then we get, by using standard elliptic estimates that

(2.32)

\displaystyle\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{6}(\Omega)}\leq C\big(\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{6}(\Omega)}+\|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{6}(\Omega)}+\|p(\rho_{\varepsilon})-p(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})\|_{L^{6}(\Omega)}\big).

Next let us estimate term by term on the right-hand side of above equation.

By applying Lemma
2.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{6}(\Omega)}\leq C\Bigl(\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{3}}\|\nabla\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{2}{3}}+\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{2}{3}}\|\nabla\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{3}}\Bigr).

Together with (
1.14
) and (
2.21
), we infer

(2.33)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\omega_{\varepsilon}|^{6}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\left(\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\|\nabla\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{4}+\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{4}\|\nabla\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{0<t<T}\big(\|\nabla\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{4}+\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\big)\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon E^{2}_{\varepsilon}(T).

While in view of (
2.21
) and (
2.24
), we have

(2.34)

\|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\nu\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\|\rho_{\varepsilon}-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\big(\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\big).

By (
2.24
) and the
R_{\varepsilon}
equation of (
2.13
), we write

\displaystyle\Delta\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle={\rm div\,}\big(\mu\Delta R_{\varepsilon}+\mu^{\prime}\nabla{\rm div\,}R_{\varepsilon}-\nabla\big(p(\rho_{\varepsilon})-p(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})\big)\big)

\displaystyle={\rm div\,}\left(\rho_{\varepsilon}\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}+\rho_{\varepsilon}R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}+\varrho_{\varepsilon}(\partial_{t}u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}+u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla u_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})-G_{\varepsilon}\right),

from which, we infer

(2.35)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle+\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\bigl\|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}+u_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\cdot\nabla u_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\|G_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+Ce^{-\alpha t}\bigl(\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\alpha t}\bigr).

Thanks to (
2.34
) and (
2.35
), we get, by applying Lemma
2.1
, that

(2.36)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}|^{6}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\big(\|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{4}+\|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{4}\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\big)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\big(\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\varepsilon\big)\big(\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\varepsilon e^{-\alpha t}\big)

\displaystyle\times\big(\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\varepsilon+\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\varepsilon e^{-\alpha t}\big)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{3}+C\varepsilon E^{2}_{\varepsilon}(T).

By inserting the estimates (
2.25
), (
2.33
) and (
2.36
) into (
2.32
), we achieve (
2.31
).
This completes the proof of Lemma
2.3
.
∎

Now we are ready to give the following lemma in order to close the energy estimates:

Lemma 2.4
.

For
T\leq T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},
there holds

(2.37)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}|^{4}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t\leq C\varepsilon^{2}+C\varepsilon E_{\varepsilon}(T),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}|^{3}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{y}\,{\rm d}t\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}+C\varepsilon E^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\varepsilon}(T).

Proof.

Indeed it follows from Proposition
2.6
, Lemma
2.3
and Hölder’s inequality that

\displaystyle\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{4}((0,T)\times\Omega)}^{4}\leq\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega)}\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{6}((0,T)\times\Omega)}^{3}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{\varepsilon}(T)\big),

\displaystyle\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}((0,T)\times\Omega)}^{3}\leq\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega)}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{6}((0,T)\times\Omega)}^{\frac{3}{2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{4}}\big(\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{4}}+\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4}}E^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\varepsilon}(T)\big).

Then (
2.37
) follow immediately.
∎

Next let us turn to the estimate of
\theta_{\varepsilon}(T).

Lemma 2.5
.

For
T\leq T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},
there holds

(2.38)

\displaystyle\theta_{\varepsilon}^{2}(T)\leq C\varepsilon+CE_{\varepsilon}(T).

Proof.

Notice that for all
T\leq T^{\star}_{\varepsilon},

0<\underline{\eta}/2\leq\rho_{\varepsilon},\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\leq 3\bar{\eta}/2,
we deduce from (
2.28
) that

\displaystyle\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{6}(T,X_{\varepsilon}(T,x,y))

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(\log\rho_{\varepsilon}-\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\bigr)^{6}(T,X_{\varepsilon}(T,x,y))

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\Big(|R_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\log\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}|^{6}+|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}|^{6}+|\varepsilon[(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}]_{\varepsilon}|^{6}\Big)(t,X_{\varepsilon}(t,x,y))\,{\rm d}t,

from which, we infer

(2.39)

\displaystyle\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}(T)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{6}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\big(\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{6}\|(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a})^{-1}\nabla\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{6}+\|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{6}+\varepsilon^{6}\|(\eta\mathfrak{w})_{y}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{6}\big){\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\big(e^{-\alpha t}\bigl(\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{6}+\varepsilon^{6}\bigr)+\|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{6}\big)\,{\rm d}t.

It follows from Sobolev embedding theorem, Proposition
2.6
and Lemma
2.3
that

(2.40)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\alpha t}\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{6}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\alpha t}\bigl(\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{6}+\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{6}(\Omega)}^{6}\bigr)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{3}+C\varepsilon E^{2}_{\varepsilon}(T).

Concerning the term related to the effective viscous flux, we get, by using Sobolev embedding inequality, that

(2.41)

\|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\big(\|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{6}(\Omega)}+\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}\big).

The estimate related to
\|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{6}(\Omega)}
is given in (
2.36
). While for
\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}
, it follows from a similar derivation of (
2.35
) that

\displaystyle\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigl(\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}}+\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}}\|\nabla u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}}\bigl\|\partial_{t}u^{\rm a}_{\varepsilon}+u_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\cdot\nabla u_{\varepsilon}^{\rm a}\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|G_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}+Ce^{-\alpha t}\bigl(\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}+\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}+\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}\bigr),

where we used the estimate of
G_{\varepsilon}
in (
2.10
). By using the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality and (
1.14
), we find

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}^{6}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{4}\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{0<t<T}\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{4}\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathfrak{D}_{t}R_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\,{\rm d}t\leq CE^{3}_{\varepsilon}(T).

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\alpha t}\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}^{6}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\alpha t}\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{4}\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{0<t<T}\|R_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{4}\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\alpha t}\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\,{\rm d}t\leq C\varepsilon^{3}.

Finally, in view of (
2.14
), one has

\displaystyle\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}\leq\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{\frac{2}{3}}\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{\frac{1}{3}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}\theta_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{3}}(t),

so that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\alpha t}\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}^{6}\,{\rm d}t\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\alpha t}\theta_{\varepsilon}^{2}(t)\,{\rm d}t\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{\varepsilon}^{2}(T).

As a result,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}^{6}\,{\rm d}t\leq C\big(E_{\varepsilon}^{3}(T)+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{\varepsilon}^{2}(T)+\varepsilon^{3}\big),

which together with (
2.36
) and (
2.41
) ensures that

(2.42)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{6}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(\|\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{6}(\Omega)}^{6}+\|\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}^{6}\bigr)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\big(E_{\varepsilon}^{3}(T)+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{\varepsilon}^{2}(T)+\varepsilon^{3}\big).

By inserting the estimates (
2.40
) and (
2.42
) into (
2.39
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\theta_{\varepsilon}^{6}(T)\leq C\big(E_{\varepsilon}^{3}(T)+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{\varepsilon}^{2}(T)+\varepsilon^{3}\big)\leq C\varepsilon^{3}+CE_{\varepsilon}^{3}(T)+\frac{\theta_{\varepsilon}^{6}(T)}{2},

which leads to (
2.38
).
∎

Now we are in a position to complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

We first deduce from (
2.22
) and (
2.37
) that

\displaystyle E_{\varepsilon}(T)\leq C\varepsilon+C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{\varepsilon}(T)\quad\mbox{for}\ T\leq T_{\varepsilon}^{\star},

which implies that for
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}
small such that that
C\varepsilon_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{1}{2},
there holds

(2.43)

E_{\varepsilon}(T)\leq C\varepsilon\quad\mbox{for}\ T\leq T_{\varepsilon}^{\star}.

This together with Lemma
2.5
ensures that

(2.44)

\theta_{\varepsilon}^{2}(T)\leq C\varepsilon\quad\mbox{for}\ T\leq T_{\varepsilon}^{\star}.

In particular, if we take
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{2}
to be small that
C\varepsilon_{2}=\frac{1}{6}\min\left\{1,\underline{\eta}\right\}^{2},

(
2.44
) contradicts with the definition of
T_{\varepsilon}^{\star}
given by (
2.14
). As a consequence,
we deduce that
T_{\varepsilon}^{\star}=T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}
.

It remains to show that the life-span
T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}=\infty
. Indeed we have shown that the estimate (
2.15
) holds
for all
t<T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}
and
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\min\{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\}
, then regularity criteria for smooth solutions of compressible Navier-Stokes equations
(see for instance
[
11
,
28
,
29
]
) ensures that
T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}=\infty.

This completes the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

∎

3.
1D compressible Navier-Stokes equations with a parameter

In this section, we investigate the 1D compressible Navier-Stokes equations (
1.6
) with a parameter
y.

We assume that
(\eta,w)
is a global smooth solution of (
1.6
) determined at the beginning of Section
2
.
For simplicity, we shall always neglect
y
variable and
denote
D_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\partial_{t}+w\partial_{x}
to be the material derivative in the rest of this section.

3.1.
Conservation of mass, momentum, and energy

Integrating (
1.6
) in
x
over
{\mathbb{T}}
leads to the conservations of the mass and of the momentum:

(3.1)

\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta(t,x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}=0,\quad\hbox{and}\quad\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)(t,x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}=0,

which together with (
1.10
) ensures that

(3.2)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta(t,x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}=1,\quad\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)(t,x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}=0,\quad\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\ y\in\mathbb{R}.

Recall the conservation of energy:

Lemma 3.1
.

There holds

(3.3)

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}

\displaystyle E_{0}(t)+\nu\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}=0\quad\hbox{with}\quad E_{0}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\bigl(\frac{1}{2}\eta w^{2}+P(\eta)\bigr)\,{\rm d}{x},\quad\hbox{and}\quad

\displaystyle P(\eta)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\frac{a}{\gamma-1}\eta^{\gamma}\quad\mbox{if $\gamma>1$}\quad\hbox{and}\quad P(\eta)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}a(\eta\log\eta+1)\quad\mbox{if $\gamma=1$}.

It follows from (
1.9
) that

(3.4)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{E}_{00}(y)&\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\bigl(\frac{1}{2}\varsigma_{0}w_{0}^{2}+(\varsigma_{0}-1)^{2}\bigr)(x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R})\quad\hbox{with}\quad\bar{\mathfrak{E}}_{00}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{E}_{00}(y),\\
E_{00}(y)&\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\bigl(\frac{1}{2}\varsigma_{0}w_{0}^{2}+P(\varsigma_{0})\bigr)(x,y)\,{\rm d}{x}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\quad\hbox{with}\quad\bar{E}_{00}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}E_{00}(y).\end{split}

Then for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\ y\in\mathbb{R}
, there holds

(3.5)

\displaystyle E_{0}(t)+\nu\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}t^{\prime}\leq E_{00}(y).

3.2.
Upper bound for the density function

We observe from (
2.2
) that the density function
\eta
admits an upper bound depending on time.
We shall derive here a time uniform upper bound for
\eta.

We first recall from
[
26
]
that for
u\in L^{1}({\mathbb{T}})
and for all
x\in{\mathbb{T}}

(3.6)

I(u)(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\int_{0}^{x}u(x^{\prime})\,{\rm d}{x}^{\prime},\quad\tilde{I}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}I(u)-\langle I(u)\rangle\quad\hbox{and}\quad\langle u\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}u(x)\,{\rm d}{x}.

It is easy to observe that
I(u)\in C({\mathbb{T}})\cap W^{1,1}({\mathbb{T}})
with

(3.7)

\|I(u)\|_{W^{1,1}_{\rm h}\cap L^{\infty}_{\rm h}}\leq\|u\|_{L^{1}_{\rm h}}.

Moreover, for each
u\in W^{1,1}({\mathbb{T}})\cap C({\mathbb{T}})
, standard density argument implies

(3.8)

\displaystyle I(u_{x})(x)=\int_{0}^{x}u_{x}\,{\rm d}{x}^{\prime}=u(x)-u(0)\quad\hbox{and}\quad\tilde{I}(u_{x})(x)=u(x)-\langle u\rangle.

Proposition 3.1
.

For all
(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times{\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R}
, there holds

(3.9)

\eta(t,x,y)\leq\max\left\{\varsigma_{0},\bar{\varsigma}_{1}\right\}\exp\bigl(4\bar{E}_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigr)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\bar{\eta},

where
\bar{\varsigma}_{0}
is given in (
2.1
) and
\bar{\varsigma}_{1}
will be determined by (
3.15
) below.

Proof.

We rewrite the momentum equation in (
1.6
) as

\displaystyle(\eta w)_{t}+(\eta w^{2})_{x}-\nu w_{xx}+p(\eta)_{x}=0.

Applying the operator
\tilde{I}
to the above equation gives

(3.10)

\tilde{I}\big((\eta w)_{t}\big)+\eta w^{2}-\nu w_{x}+p(\eta)-\langle\eta w^{2}-\nu w_{x}+p(\eta)\rangle=0.

We first compute

\displaystyle\tilde{I}\big((\eta w)_{t}\big)=\partial_{t}\tilde{I}(\eta w)=D_{t}\tilde{I}(\eta w)-w\partial_{x}\tilde{I}(\eta w)=D_{t}\tilde{I}(\eta w)-\eta w^{2}.

While it follows from the transport equation of (
1.6
) that

\displaystyle-w_{x}=\frac{-\eta w_{x}}{\eta}=\frac{\eta_{t}+w\eta_{x}}{\eta}=D_{t}\log\eta.

By inserting the above equalities into (
3.10
) and
the fact that
\langle w_{x}\rangle=0
, we obtain

(3.11)

D_{t}\tilde{I}(\eta w)+\nu D_{t}\log\eta+p(\eta)-\langle\eta w^{2}+p(\eta)\rangle=0.

Let
{X}(t,x,y)
be the trajectory of
w,
which is determined by

(3.12)

\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}X_{w}(t,x,y)=w(t,X(t,x,y));\quad X_{w}(0,x,y)=x.

Let
\mathfrak{y}(t,x,y)=\log\eta(t,X_{w}(t,x,y),y)
. Then in view of (
3.11
), we write

(3.13)

\nu\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\mathfrak{y}=g(\mathfrak{y})+\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\mathfrak{b}

with

g(\mathfrak{y})=-p(\eta)+\langle\eta w^{2}+p(\eta)\rangle,\quad\mathfrak{b}=-\tilde{I}(\eta w).

Before proceeding, we recall the following lemma:

Lemma 3.2

(Lemma 1.3 of
[
27
]
)
.

Suppose
g\in C(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{y},b\in W^{1,1}(0,T)
for all
T>0
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{y}
verifies

\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\mathfrak{y}=g(\mathfrak{y})+\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\mathfrak{b}\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{+},\quad\mathfrak{y}(0)=\mathfrak{y}_{0}.

If
g(+\infty)=-\infty
and there exist non-negative constants
N_{0},N_{1}\geq 0
so that
for any
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\infty,

\mathfrak{b}(t_{2})-\mathfrak{b}(t_{1})\leq N_{0}+N_{1}(t_{2}-t_{1}).

Then one has

\mathfrak{y}(t)\leq\bar{\mathfrak{y}}<\infty,\quad\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\quad\hbox{with}\quad\bar{\mathfrak{y}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\max\{\mathfrak{y}_{0},\mathfrak{y}_{1}\}+N_{0},

where
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
is such that
g(\mathfrak{y})\leq-N_{1}
for all
\mathfrak{y}\geq\mathfrak{y}_{1}
.

Now we would like to apply Lemma
3.2
to derive an upper bound of
\eta
via (
3.13
). Firstly, it follows from
(
3.5
) and (
3.4
) that

g(\mathfrak{y})=-p(e^{\mathfrak{y}})+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\left(\eta w^{2}+p(\eta)\right)\,{\rm d}{x}\leq-p(e^{\mathfrak{y}})+\gamma\bar{E}_{00}\to-\infty,\quad\mbox{as}\ \mathfrak{y}\to+\infty.

While in view of (
3.6
), we have for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

(3.14)

|{\tilde{I}}(\eta w)|_{L^{\infty}_{\rm h}}\leq 2\|\eta w\|_{L^{1}_{\rm h}}\leq 2\|\eta\|_{L^{1}_{\rm h}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\eta w^{2}\|_{L^{1}_{\rm h}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 2\bar{E}_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}},

which implies

{|\tilde{I}(\eta w)(t_{2})-\tilde{I}(\eta w)(t_{1})|\leq 4E_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}},\ \forall\,t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,\infty).}

On the other hand, we observe that

(3.15)

-p(\bar{\varsigma}_{1})+\gamma\bar{E}_{00}=0\Rightarrow\bar{\varsigma}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\Big(\frac{\gamma\bar{E}_{00}}{a}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}.

Hence we get, by applying Lemma
3.2
, that

(3.16)

\mathfrak{y}\leq\max\{\log\bar{\varsigma}_{0},\log\bar{\varsigma}_{1}\}+4E_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
\bar{\varsigma}_{0}
is given in (
2.1
). This leads to (
3.9
).
∎

3.3.
Decay estimates of
L^{2}
norms

With the upper bound of
\eta
obtained in (
3.9
), similarly to Lemma 2.1 of
[
32
]
, we have

Lemma 3.3
.

Under the assumptions of (
1.10
), one has

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\leq\bar{\eta}^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

Proof.

Under the assumptions of (
1.10
), one has (
3.2
) for all
t>0.
Then we deduce from
(
3.9
) and Poincaré’s inequality that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}-\langle\eta w\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta(x)\eta(x^{\prime})|w(x)-w(x^{\prime})|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\bar{\eta}^{2}}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w(x)-w(x^{\prime})|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bar{\eta}^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w-\langle w\rangle|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\leq\bar{\eta}^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

∎

By combining Lemma
3.3
with the energy estimate (
3.5
), we achieve

(3.17)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}t\leq\bar{\eta}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\,{\rm d}t\leq\bar{\eta}^{2}\nu^{-1}E_{00}.

Proposition 3.2
.

There exist positive constants
\alpha>0
and
C
which depend on
(a,\gamma,\nu,\bar{\varsigma}_{0},\bar{E}_{00})
such that

(3.18)

\displaystyle\|\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}w(t)\|_{L^{2}_{\rm h}}+\|(\eta-1)(t)\|_{L^{2}_{\rm h}}\leq CE_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}}(y)e^{-\alpha t},\quad\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\ y\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

By
combining (
3.1
) with (
3.3
), we obtain

(3.19)

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\eta w^{2}+\big(P(\eta)-P(1)-P^{\prime}(1)(\eta-1)\big)\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}+\nu\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}=0.

While we get, by using Taylor’s formula, that

P(\eta)-P(1)-P^{\prime}(1)(\eta-1)=P^{\prime\prime}(\hat{\eta})(\eta-1)^{2}=\frac{p^{\prime}(\hat{\eta})}{\hat{\eta}}(\eta-1)^{2}=a\gamma\hat{\eta}^{\gamma-2}(\eta-1)^{2},

for some
\hat{\eta}
between
\eta
and
1
. Due to the upper bound
0\leq\eta\leq\bar{\eta}<\infty
, we have

0\leq\hat{\eta}\leq\max\{1,\bar{\eta}\}\leq 1+\bar{\eta}.

So that if
\gamma\in[1,2]
, we have

(3.20)

\displaystyle P(\eta)-P(1)-P^{\prime}(1)(\eta-1)\geq a\gamma(1+\bar{\eta})^{\gamma-2}(\eta-1)^{2}.

While for
\gamma\in[1,2]
, it is easy to observe that

(3.21)

\displaystyle P(\eta)-P(1)-P^{\prime}(1)(\eta-1)\leq C(\gamma)a(\eta-1)^{2},

where the constant
C
is solely determined by
\gamma
. It follows from (
3.20
) and (
3.21
) that
P(\eta)-P(1)-P^{\prime}(1)(\eta-1)
behaves like
(\eta-1)^{2}
.

Notice that
I(\eta-1)|_{x=0}=I(\eta-1)|_{x=1}=0.
By multiplying the momentum equation of (
1.6
) by
I(\eta-1)

and using integration by parts, one has

(3.22)

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)_{t}I(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w^{2})(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}+\nu\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}.

We first compute

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)_{t}I(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}=\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)I(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)\partial_{t}I(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}.

By virtue of (
3.2
) and Lemma
3.3
, one has

\displaystyle\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)\partial_{t}I(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)I((\eta w)_{x})\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)(\eta w(t,x)-\eta w(t,0))\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|\leq\bar{\eta}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\leq\bar{\eta}^{3}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

Similarly, we have

(3.23)

\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w^{2})(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|\leq(1+\bar{\eta})\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\leq(1+\bar{\eta})\bar{\eta}^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

While applying Young’s inequality yields

\nu\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|\leq\delta\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|\eta-1|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\ +\delta^{-1}\nu^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

By inserting the above estimates into (
3.3
), we find

(3.24)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle-\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)I(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle\leq\delta\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|\eta-1|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\big(\bar{\eta}^{2}+\bar{\eta}^{3}+\delta^{-1}\nu^{2}\big)\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

Notice that as long as
\gamma\geq 1
, one has

\big(p(r)-p(1)\big)(r-1)-a(r-1)^{2}=ar(r^{\gamma-1}-1)(r-1)\geq 0,\quad\forall\,r\geq 0.

As a result, it comes out

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\big(p(\eta)-p(1)\big)(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}\geq a\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta-1)^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

Then choosing
\delta=\frac{a}{2}
in (
3.24
) yields

(3.25)

\displaystyle\frac{a}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta-1)^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}-\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)I(\eta-1)\,{\rm d}{x}\leq\big(\bar{\eta}^{2}+\bar{\eta}^{3}+2a^{-1}\nu^{2}\big)\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

Multiplying (
3.19
) by a constant
A_{1}
and summing up the resulting inequality with (
3.25
), we get

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Bigl(\frac{A_{1}}{2}\eta w^{2}+A_{1}\big(P(\eta)-P(1)-P^{\prime}(1)(\eta-1)\big)-(\eta w)I(\eta-1)\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle+A_{1}\nu\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\frac{a}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta-1)^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\leq\big(\bar{\eta}^{2}+\bar{\eta}^{3}+2a^{-1}\nu^{2}\big)\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

Taking
A_{1}
large enough so that

(3.26)

\displaystyle A_{1}\geq 4,\quad A_{1}a\gamma(1+\bar{\eta})^{\gamma-2}\geq 2,\quad A_{1}\nu\geq\bar{\eta}^{2}+\bar{\eta}^{3}+2a^{-1}\nu^{2}+1,

we obtain

(3.27)

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Bigl(\frac{A_{1}}{2}\eta w^{2}+A_{1}\big(P(\eta)-P(1)-P^{\prime}(1)(\eta-1)\big)

\displaystyle-(\eta w)I(\eta-1)\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\frac{a}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta-1)^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\leq 0.

Furthermore, due to

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\bigl|(\eta w)I(\eta-1)\bigr|\,{\rm d}{x}\leq\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|\eta w|\,{\rm d}{x}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|\eta-1|\,{\rm d}{x}\leq\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|\eta-1|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x},

and taking into account (
3.20
) and (
3.21
), we have

(3.28)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Bigl(\frac{A_{1}}{2}\eta w^{2}+A_{1}\big(P(\eta)-P(1)

\displaystyle-P^{\prime}(1)(\eta-1)\big)-(\eta w)I(\eta-1)\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Bigl(\frac{A_{1}}{4}\eta w^{2}+\frac{A_{1}}{2}a\gamma(1+\bar{\eta})^{\gamma-2}(\eta-1)^{2}\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x},

and

(3.29)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Bigl(\frac{A_{1}}{2}\eta w^{2}+A_{1}\big(P(\eta)-P(1)

\displaystyle-P^{\prime}(1)(\eta-1)\big)-(\eta w)I(\eta-1)\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Bigl(\big(\frac{A_{1}}{2}+1\big)\eta w^{2}+\big(A_{1}aC(\gamma)+1\big)(\eta-1)^{2}\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}.

Then (
3.18
) follows from (
3.27
)–(
3.29
) and Lemma
3.3
.
∎

3.4.
Lower bound of the density function

Proposition 3.3
.

There exists a positive constant
\underline{\eta}
which depends on
(a,\gamma,\nu,\bar{\varsigma}_{0},\underline{\varsigma}_{0},\bar{E}_{00})
so that

(3.30)

\eta(t,x,y)\geq\underline{\eta},\quad\forall\,(t,x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times{\mathbb{T}}\times\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

Let

\mathfrak{y}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\log\frac{1}{\eta}=-\log\eta.

Then it follows from (
3.11
) that

\displaystyle D_{t}\big(\nu\mathfrak{y}_{1}-\tilde{I}(\eta w)\big)=p(\eta)-\langle\eta w^{2}+p(\eta)\rangle,

from which, we deduce that for each
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\max_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}\big(\nu\mathfrak{y}_{1}-\tilde{I}(\eta w)\big)(t,\cdot)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}\big(\nu\mathfrak{y}_{1}-\tilde{I}(\eta w)\big)(0,\cdot)+\int_{0}^{t}\max_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}}\big(p(\eta)+\langle\eta w^{2}+p(\eta)\rangle\big){\rm d}t^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq-\nu\log\underline{\varsigma}_{0}+E_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}}+(a\bar{\eta}^{\gamma}+\gamma E_{00})t.

This implies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{y}_{1}(t)\leq-\log\underline{\varsigma}_{0}+2\nu^{-1}E_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\nu^{-1}(a\bar{\eta}^{\gamma}+\gamma E_{00})t,

which is equivalent to

(3.31)

\displaystyle\eta(t)

\displaystyle\geq\underline{\varsigma}_{0}\exp\big(-2\nu^{-1}\bar{E}_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\nu^{-1}(a\bar{\eta}^{\gamma}+\gamma\bar{E}_{00})t\big)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\underline{\eta}_{1}(t).

To obtain a lower bound of the density function for large time, we
define

\mathfrak{y}_{2}=\exp(\lambda(\nu\mathfrak{y}_{1}-\tilde{I}(\eta w))),\quad\lambda>0

which solves

\displaystyle D_{t}\mathfrak{y}_{2}

\displaystyle=\lambda\exp(\lambda(\nu\mathfrak{y}_{1}-\tilde{I}(\eta w)))\big(p(\eta)-\langle\eta w^{2}+p(\eta)\rangle\big)

\displaystyle=-\lambda\mathfrak{y}_{2}\langle\eta w^{2}+p(\eta)\rangle+\lambda\eta^{-\lambda\nu}a\eta^{\gamma}\exp(-\lambda\tilde{I}(\eta w)).

Taking
\lambda
so that
\lambda\nu=\gamma
gives rise to

(3.32)

\displaystyle D_{t}\mathfrak{y}_{2}+\nu^{-1}\gamma\mathfrak{y}_{2}\langle\eta w^{2}+p(\eta)\rangle=a\nu^{-1}\gamma\exp(-\lambda\tilde{I}(\eta w)).

Yet we observe from Proposition
3.2
that

(3.33)

\displaystyle\langle p(\eta)\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)\,{\rm d}{x}\geq p(1)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|p(\eta)-p(1)|\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle\geq p(1)-p^{\prime}(\bar{\eta})\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|\eta-1|\,{\rm d}{x}\geq a-a\gamma\bar{\eta}^{\gamma-1}\big(Ce^{-\alpha t}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Let
T_{1}
be such that

(3.34)

\displaystyle\gamma\bar{\eta}^{\gamma-1}\big(Ce^{-\alpha T_{1}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{1}{2}.

Then for all
t\geq T_{1}
, there holds

\langle\eta w^{2}+p(\eta)\rangle\geq\langle p(\eta)\rangle\geq\frac{a}{2},

so that we deduce from (
3.32
) that for
t\geq T_{1}
,

(3.35)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{y}_{2}(t)\leq e^{-\tilde{a}t}\mathfrak{y}_{2}(T_{1})+\int_{T_{1}}^{t}e^{-\tilde{a}(t-t^{\prime})}a\nu^{-1}\gamma\exp(-\lambda\tilde{I}(\eta w))(t^{\prime})\,{\rm d}t^{\prime},

where
\tilde{a}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\frac{a\nu^{-1}\gamma}{2}>0
. Together with (
3.14
), (
3.35
) ensures that
for
t\geq T_{1}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{y}_{2}(t)\leq e^{-\tilde{a}t}\mathfrak{y}_{2}(T_{1})+a\left(\tilde{a}\nu\right)^{-1}\gamma e^{2\lambda E_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}}},

from
which, we infer

\displaystyle\eta^{-\lambda\gamma}(t)\leq e^{-\tilde{a}t}\eta^{-\lambda\gamma}(T_{1})e^{2\lambda E_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+a\left(\tilde{a}\nu\right)^{-1}\gamma e^{4\lambda E_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}}},

that is,
for
t\geq T_{1}
there holds

(3.36)

\displaystyle\eta(t)\geq\underline{\eta}_{2}(T_{1})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\Big(e^{-\tilde{a}T_{1}}\underline{\eta}_{1}^{-\lambda\gamma}(T_{1})e^{2\lambda\bar{E}_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+a\left(\tilde{a}\nu\right)^{-1}\gamma e^{4\lambda\bar{E}_{00}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big)^{-\frac{1}{\lambda\gamma}}.

Combining (
3.31
) and (
3.36
), we deduce that (
3.30
) holds with

(3.37)

\displaystyle\underline{\eta}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\min\left\{\underline{\eta}_{1}(T_{1}),\underline{\eta}_{2}(T_{1})\right\}.

This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

3.5.
Decay estimate of
\|w_{x}(t)\|_{L^{2}_{\rm h}}

It follows from (
1.9
) that

(3.38)

E_{10}(y)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\|\varsigma_{0}-1\|_{H^{1}_{\rm h}}^{2}+\|w_{0}\|_{H^{1}_{\rm h}}^{2}\in(L^{1}\cap L^{\infty})(\mathbb{R})\quad\hbox{and}\quad\bar{E}_{10}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}E_{10}(y)<\infty.

We start with the following two lemmas:

Lemma 3.4
.

There holds for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

(3.39)

\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w^{4}\,{\rm d}{x}+6\nu\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w|^{2}|w_{x}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\leq 24a\nu^{-1}\bar{\eta}^{2\gamma-1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

Proof.

Multiplying
4w^{3}
to the momentum equation of (
1.6
) and integrating the resulting equation over
{\mathbb{T}}
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w^{4}\,{\rm d}{x}+12\nu\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w|^{2}|w_{x}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle=12\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)w^{2}w_{x}\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle\leq 6\nu\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w|^{2}|w_{x}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+24a\nu^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta^{2\gamma}w^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle\leq 6\nu\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w|^{2}|w_{x}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+24a\nu^{-1}\bar{\eta}^{2\gamma-1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x},

where we used Lemma
3.3
in the last step. And (
3.39
) follows.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

There exist positive constants
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
solely depending on
(a,\gamma,\nu,\bar{\varsigma}_{0},\bar{E}_{00})
, so that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

(3.40)

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Bigl(\frac{\nu}{2}w_{x}^{2}+\frac{a^{2}}{2\nu}\big(\eta^{2\gamma}

\displaystyle-1-2\gamma(\eta-1)\big)-\big(p(\eta)-p(1)\big)w_{x}\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w_{t}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle\leq 5\bar{\eta}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w|^{2}|w_{x}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+B_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+B_{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta-1)^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

Proof.

Multiplying
w_{t}
to the momentum equation of (
1.6
) and integrating the resulting equation over
{\mathbb{T}}
gives

(3.41)

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)_{t}w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w^{2})_{x}w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}+\frac{\nu}{2}\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)_{x}w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}=0.

We now handle term by term above. Firstly, it follows from the continuity equation of (
1.6
) that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)_{t}w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w_{t}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w^{2})_{x}w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta ww_{x}w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}.

This implies

(3.42)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w)_{t}w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta w^{2})_{x}w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w_{t}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta ww_{x}w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w_{t}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}-4\bar{\eta}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w|^{2}|w_{x}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

It is rather complicated to estimate the term related to the pressure in (
3.41
). We compute

(3.43)

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)_{x}w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}=-\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\big(p(\eta)-p(1)\big)w_{x}\,{\rm d}{x}+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)_{t}w_{x}\,{\rm d}{x}.

For the last term of (
3.43
), we decompose it as

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)_{t}w_{x}\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle=\nu^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)_{t}\big(\nu w_{x}-p(\eta)\big)\,{\rm d}{x}+\nu^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)_{t}p(\eta)\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle=\nu^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)w\big(\nu w_{xx}-p(\eta)_{x}\big)\,{\rm d}{x}-a(\gamma-1)\nu^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta^{\gamma}w_{x}\big(\nu w_{x}-p(\eta)\big)\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{a^{2}}{2\nu}\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\big(\eta^{2\gamma}-1-2\gamma(\eta-1)\big)\,{\rm d}{x}.

Observing that

\displaystyle\nu^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)w\big(\nu w_{xx}-p(\eta)_{x}\big)\,{\rm d}{x}=\nu^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)w\eta\big(w_{t}+ww_{x})\big)\,{\rm d}{x}.

Applying Young’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\nu^{-1}\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)w\eta w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w_{t}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+4\nu^{-2}a\bar{\eta}^{2\gamma+1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x},

\displaystyle\nu^{-1}\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)w\eta ww_{x}\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq a^{2}\nu^{-2}\bar{\eta}^{2\gamma+2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\bar{\eta}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w|^{2}|w_{x}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x},

where we used Lemma
3.3
in the second inequality.

We finally compute

\displaystyle a(\gamma-1)\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta^{\gamma}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|\leq a(\gamma-1)\bar{\eta}^{\gamma}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x},

and

\displaystyle\nu^{-1}a(\gamma-1)\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta^{\gamma}w_{x}p(\eta)\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\nu^{-1}a(\gamma-1)\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta^{2\gamma}-1)w_{x}\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\nu^{-1}a(\gamma-1)\Big(\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta^{2\gamma}-1)^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\nu^{-1}a(\gamma-1)\Big(\bigl(2\gamma\bar{\eta}^{2\gamma-1}\bigr)^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta-1)^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\Big).

By inserting the above estimates into (
3.43
), we achieve

(3.44)

\begin{split}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}p(\eta)_{x}w_{t}\,{\rm d}{x}\geq&\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Bigl(\frac{a^{2}}{2\nu}\big(\eta^{2\gamma}-1-2\gamma(\eta-1)\big)-\big(p(\eta)-p(1)\big)w_{x}\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}\\
&-\Bigl(\bar{\eta}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w|^{2}|w_{x}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\frac{1}{4}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w_{t}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+B_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+B_{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta-1)^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\Bigr),\end{split}

where

\displaystyle B_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}4\nu^{-2}a\bar{\eta}^{2\gamma+1}+\nu^{-2}a^{2}\bar{\eta}^{2\gamma+2}+a(\gamma-1)\bigl(\bar{\eta}^{\gamma}+\nu^{-1}\bigr)\quad\hbox{and}\quad B_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}4a\gamma^{2}(\gamma-1)\nu^{-1}\bar{\eta}^{2(2\gamma-1)}.

By substituting (
3.42
) and (
3.44
) into (
3.41
), we achieve (
3.40
).
∎

By multiplying (
3.39
) by a large enough positive constant
A_{2},
which
depends on
(\nu,\bar{\eta}),
and summing up the resulting inequality
with (
3.40
), we obtain

Corollary 3.1
.

Let
A_{2}
and
B_{3}
be determined by

\displaystyle 6A_{2}\nu=5\bar{\eta}+1,\quad B_{3}=B_{1}+24A_{2}\nu^{-1}a\bar{\eta}^{\gamma+1}.

There holds

(3.45)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Bigl(A_{2}\eta w^{4}+\nu w_{x}^{2}+\frac{a^{2}}{2\nu}\big(\eta^{2\gamma}-1-2\gamma(\eta-1)\big)-\big(p(\eta)-p(1)\big)w_{x}\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\eta w_{t}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w|^{2}|w_{x}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}\leq B_{3}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}w_{x}^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+B_{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}(\eta-1)^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

Proposition 3.4
.

There exist positive constants
\alpha
and
C
solely depending on
(a,\gamma,\nu,\bar{\varsigma}_{0},\underline{\varsigma}_{0},\bar{E}_{00})
such that

(3.46)

\displaystyle\|w(t)\|_{H^{1}_{\rm h}}+\|w(t)\|_{C^{0,\frac{1}{2}}_{\rm h}}\leq CE_{10}^{\frac{1}{2}}(y)e^{-\alpha t},\quad\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\ y\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

Thanks to (
3.27
) and (
3.45
), we deduce that
there exists a large enough positive constant
A_{3}
depending only on
(a,\gamma,\nu,\bar{\varsigma}_{0},\bar{E}_{00})
so that

(3.47)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\frac{\rm d}{{\rm d}t}F_{2}(t)+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\bigl(w_{x}^{2}+(\eta-1)^{2}+|w|^{2}|w_{x}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\eta w_{t}^{2}\bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}\leq 0,

where

(3.48)

\displaystyle F_{2}(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\hbox{\footnotesize def}}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Bigl(

\displaystyle\frac{A_{1}A_{3}}{2}\eta w^{2}+A_{1}A_{3}\big(P(\eta)-P(1)-P^{\prime}(1)(\eta-1)\big)-A_{3}(\eta w)I(\eta-1)

\displaystyle+A_{2}\eta w^{4}+\nu w_{x}^{2}+\frac{a^{2}}{2\nu}\big(\eta^{2\gamma}-1-2\gamma(\eta-1)\big)-\big(p(\eta)-p(1)\big)w_{x}\Bigr)\,{\rm d}{x}.

Observing that

\displaystyle\bigl|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\big(p(\eta)-p(1)\big)w_{x}\,{\rm d}{x}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq(2\nu)^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|p(\eta)-p(1)|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w_{x}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}

\displaystyle\leq(2\nu)^{-1}p^{\prime}(\bar{\eta})^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|\eta-1|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}|w_{x}|^{2}\,{\rm d}{x}.

By choosing
A_{3}
sufficiently large, we have

(3.49)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\left(\eta w^{2}+(\eta-1)^{2}+\eta w^{4}+w_{x}^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}{x}\leq F_{2}\leq C\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\left(\eta w^{2}+(\eta-1)^{2}+\eta w^{4}+w_{x}^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}{x},

where
C
solely depends on
(a,\gamma,\nu,\bar{\varsigma}_{0},\bar{E}_{00})
. Then (
3.46
) follows from (
3.47
),
(
3.49
) and (
3.18
).
∎

We remark that up to now, the strictly lower bound of
\eta
in (
3.30
) is not really needed.
Indeed let us recall

Lemma 3.6

(Lemma 3.2 in
[
6
]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded domain with
d\geq 1.
Let
\rho
be a non-negative function satisfying

\int_{\Omega}\rho\,{\rm d}{x}\geq M>0\quad\hbox{and}\quad\int_{\Omega}\rho^{q}\,{\rm d}{x}\leq E_{0}<\infty,

for some
q>1.
Then for each
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
, there holds
